# soccer-gm

A client-side, BBGM-style soccer management sim (TypeScript, React, Vite, IndexedDB). All milestones M0-M6 are done and merged (see "Milestone status" below), and a great deal has shipped since.

**This file is the current record.** The other documents in the repo are narrower, and one is deliberately historical:

| Doc | What it is |
| --- | --- |
| `SOCCER_GM_SPEC.md` | The original 2026-07-08 build brief. **Historical** — §4–§5 are still the definitive statement of how the match engine works and §8 still holds the validation gates, but its world/tactics/roadmap sections describe a game that diverged. Its own header box lists where. |
| `DESIGN.md` / `PRODUCT.md` | The visual design system ("Front Office Terminal") and the product/brand brief. Implemented; `src/ui/styles.css` is the source of truth for tokens. |
| `docs/finance-design.md` | Design intent of the finance layer. Accurate on structure, not a ledger of constants. |
| `docs/transfer-mobility.md` | Postmortem: how the strength ladder inverted, and the need-buy findings that followed. |
| `docs/save-performance-plan.md` | The split-player-storage plan and what it measured. Phases 0-2 shipped. |
| `docs/player-save-findings.md` | **A live work queue** from six real player saves. Fixed items carry a FIXED banner; unmarked items are still open. |
| `docs/eafc-import.md` | The dev-only EA FC roster converter. Never ships. |
| `docs/crazygames.md` | How to build and upload the CrazyGames bundle. |
| `docs/github-pages.md` | The GitHub Pages deploy: base path, SPA fallback, and how to verify one. |
| `docs/cloudflare.md` | How worldsoccersim.org actually deploys (it deploys itself), the lag, and how to check what's live. |
| `docs/analytics.md` | PostHog dashboards and pointers; `src/ui/analytics.ts` owns the event set. |

> **This file is a condensed ledger, not a changelog.** Blow-by-blow constant-retune history (dated before/after values, dynasty-audit numbers) lives in git history and the `docs/` design specs — don't re-add it here. Keep entries to *what a feature is, where it lives, and its non-obvious gotchas.*

## Commands

```bash
npm run dev        # Vite dev server
npm run build      # production build
npm test           # vitest run (full suite)
npm run test:watch # vitest watch
npm run test:cluster # split the suite across two Macs (falls back to local)
npm run test:clean-worlds # prune stale world-fixture caches (see below; --all / --dry-run)
npm run typecheck  # tsc --noEmit
npm run cli        # scripts/cli.ts (headless sim harness for audits)
```

**Which to reach for when a run will be long.** There are two Macs available to this project (see `.test-cluster.example.json`), and the two `:cluster` commands use the second one. Measured 2026-08-29: the full suite is **1384s on one machine, 570s across two — 2.43x**. **Those two numbers predate the 626-club world and are low**: total CI work rose 49% with it (5615s → 8367s of runner time), so expect roughly half as long again on both until someone re-times them — the *ratio* is what the advice rests on and that is unaffected. So for a *full-suite* run or a *multi-seed audit*, prefer `npm run test:cluster` / `npm run audit:cluster` over the plain command. Both **fall back to a normal local run within ~2s** when the other machine is asleep, offline or unconfigured, so neither is a gamble. **`.test-cluster.json` is gitignored, and a git worktree only checks out TRACKED files, so it is never present in one** — which made "no config here" a guaranteed false negative for any session working in a worktree, and `testCluster.sh` compounded it by copying the blank example over the top and quietly running local (measured 2026-08-30: three full-suite runs at 1x while the other Mac sat idle). Both scripts now resolve the config from the **main checkout** via `git rev-parse --git-common-dir` when it is not beside them, and say so — plain `npm test` stays right for a scoped run of a few files, where the sync and startup cost more than they save. **Two caveats worth knowing before making it reflex.** (a) They `rsync --delete` the working tree onto the other machine, so **whichever machine you invoke from is the source of truth for that run**; the target is a scratch dir (`~/soccer-gm-testcluster`), never a real checkout, but do not treat this as two-way sync. (b) **The other Mac may have its own session running** — observed live on 2026-08-29, a full vitest run under way there, back when a second account worked this repo; one developer with two machines can do the same to themselves — so a cluster run can land on a machine that is already busy, and will both go slower and slow the other session down. Probing whether the other machine is idle is cheap; assuming it is, is not.
Audit scripts live in `scripts/` (e.g. `divisionAudit.ts`, `weakLeaguesAudit.ts`) — run with `tsx`.

**An audit's seeds can be split across two Macs (`npm run audit:cluster -- <auditName>`, `scripts/auditCluster.sh`, 2026-08-28).** Audits are the long pole — longer than the test suite: `weakLeaguesAudit` defaults to `SEASONS=20 × SEEDS=1,2,3,4`, i.e. 80 season-cycles at ~13s each (`scripts/worldGenSplitProbe.ts` measures that 13s), and this file refers to 30- and 100-season runs elsewhere. 13 scripts read `SEEDS` and loop them **serially**. Seeds split where test files often can't: a seed is an independent world, whereas the slow *test* files chain seasons (season N+1 starts from season N) and are serial by nature — the distinction to keep straight before trying to parallelise the wrong thing. Shares `.test-cluster.json` (host, capacities) with the test runner, and the same guards: login shell for the remote's PATH, tree-hash check after rsync so half your results can't come from different code, and fall back to running every seed locally when the other machine is unreachable. **The load-bearing part is that it REFUSES to split the six audits that aggregate across seeds** — `weakLeaguesAudit`, `leagueVolumeProbe`, `positionAwardAudit`, `midVsMidProbe`, `totsSlotProbe`, `totsWeightProbe`. Those don't just loop and print; they average over the seeds and gate on the mean, precisely because one seed is noisier than the effect (`weakLeaguesAudit`'s own header: adjacent rungs "coin-flip on seed noise", and it prints a NOTE that the mean gate is under-powered below 3 seeds). Split in half, each machine averages its own two seeds and prints a confident-looking number over a sample the script itself calls too small — a plausible wrong answer, not a crash, which is the same failure class as a distributed test run going green against stale code. `--per-seed-only` splits anyway when you want the per-seed sections and are ignoring the aggregate; `--local` runs every seed here with the aggregate intact. **`FORWARD_VARS` in the script is a hand-maintained list of the env knobs audits read** (`SEASONS`/`SEED`/`SHOW`/`RUN`/`RATE`/`N`/`ALL_N`) — a new audit reading a new variable silently won't have it reach the remote half until it is added there.

**Open work queue from real player saves: `docs/player-save-findings.md`.** Six saves sent in by players were audited 2026-08-17; that file is a *fix queue*, not a design doc — each item carries evidence, root cause with line refs, fix shape and gating, and they're independent of each other. Two things there generalize beyond the individual bugs: **(a) real players reach season 75**, roughly triple the 20-30 season horizon every dynasty audit in `scripts/` gates at, and at least one invariant (the country strength ladder) fails only out there while the 20-season gate stays green; **(b) `scripts/playerSaveAudit.ts`** audits any exported save for size/integrity/balance, and reads the **raw parsed file rather than going through `migrateLeague`** — migration repairs and culls, so a migrated league tells you what the game would fix, not what the player has. Player saves live in `leaguesaves/` (gitignored, other people's data, never committed) and exist only on the machine they were sent to.

**EA FC roster converter** (`scripts/eafcRosterConvert.ts` + `scripts/eafc/`, docs in `docs/eafc-import.md`): turns an EA FC player CSV into a roster file for the Leagues → Import Custom League flow, for playing with real clubs/players. Dev-only and **never shipped or committed** — the CSV and generated JSON are gitignored, the game keeps shipping fictional clubs (trademark caution). The load-bearing part is the rescale: EA overalls are **rank-matched onto a freshly generated world's OVR distribution** rather than used raw (a generated world spans ~23-81 and only reaches 90+ via progression; EA's top flights run 47-91), so the imported world carries the same OVR distribution a generated one does and the anti-inflation equilibrium is untouched. Per-skill ratings come from EA's attributes (the two sets are both 1-99 and mostly namesakes) then get uniformly shifted to hit the rescaled overall, so players keep their individuality; potential gets its *own* curve (different distribution — reusing the overall curve gave every wonderkid an identical ceiling). **Leagues are matched by `league_id`, never by name**: names collide across federations and the exports drop the country prefix ("Bundesliga" is Germany's *and* Austria's, "Premier League" England's *and* Ukraine's), which silently imported 12 Austrian clubs into the German top flight — `scripts/eafc/inspectLeagues.ts` audits a dataset for exactly this before you trust it. **Coverage is every tier-1 and tier-2 competition (24 of the world's 28)** — the big four's third divisions are deliberately uncovered, since rules for them would be written from real league names with no dataset to verify against, and a test pins that they stay uncovered so it reads as a decision rather than an oversight **Verified against the real FC26 export on 2026-08-29, and two of the four newest leagues were importing NOTHING** — a name rule that matches nothing and one that matches the wrong country both present as an empty competition. Scotland's cell there is a bare `Premiership` (correctly left unclaimed, since Northern Ireland's NIFL would take it) and Greece's is a bare `Super League` that **four** federations in that one file share (Greece 63, China 2012, Switzerland 189, India 2149), so the ambiguity guard dropped all four. Both are now reached by **id** (50, 63; plus the Netherlands' 10) and the bare `super_league` pattern is gone — Belgium's `Pro League` situation exactly. **A partly-covered league is scaled against the pooled world, not its own band** (`PARTIAL_LEAGUE_COVERAGE` 0.75): rank-matching maps the source's *weakest* player onto the reference league's weakest, so FC26's 4-of-14 Greek clubs — the four best in the country — came out stating Panathinaikos were relegation fodder (top-11 56.7 against 62.6 pooled). The pooled curve only protects because the pool holds other leagues; a one-league conversion is its own pool. Slot counts now come from `competitionTeamCount` rather than a flat 20, so an over-full league is cut by squad strength here instead of by slot order in the importer. (Belgium/Turkey added 2026-08-12, the Netherlands/Greece/Scotland/Serbia 2026-08-28; `COVERED_COMPETITIONS` is derived from the rules so the "which divisions stay fictional" report can't drift, and a test now asserts it covers every competition `worldCompetitions()` ships). **A third trap: `LEAGUE_RULES` ORDER is load-bearing, not cosmetic.** `mapLeague` returns on the *first* matching rule, so a tier-2 name that *contains* another rule's pattern must be listed above it. Two live cases: "Scottish Championship" contains England's bare `championship` (listed after England it silently imports every Scottish second-tier club into the English Championship — the same failure as the 12 Austrian clubs), and "Super League 2" contains Greece's own `super_league`. Both second tiers therefore sit *above* the rule they would otherwise be eaten by, and tests pin both directions. Three bare names are deliberately left unclaimed because other countries share them: `premiership` (Northern Ireland's NIFL), `prva_liga` (a *top* flight in Croatia/Slovenia/Bosnia/Montenegro while being Serbia's second tier) and unqualified `superliga` (Denmark's). **The Dutch, Greek, Scottish and Serbian rules have no verified ids** — they were written from real league names, not confirmed against a dataset, so they resolve via the unambiguous-name fallback exactly like Portugal's second tier; run `inspectLeagues.ts` over a real export and add ids to `LEAGUE_IDS` before trusting an import covering any of them. Two traps that generalize: **(a)** FC26 has *three* leagues named exactly "Pro League" — Belgium's (4), Saudi Arabia's (350), the UAE's (2013) — so Belgium has **no bare name rule at all** and is reached by id only; a `pro_league` pattern would have put Al Hilal in the Belgian top flight. **(b)** `normalizeLeague` **must not fold accents**, however much `s_per_lig` ("Süper Lig") looks like a bug: folding was tried and measured to hand Brazil's "Série A" (14 clubs) to Italy and "Primera División" to Spain, since both then match a pattern *and* are unique names in the file, so the unambiguous-name fallback trusts them — 19 foreign clubs, silently. Turkey carries the mangled form as a pattern instead. No dataset seen so far has Belgium's, Turkey's or Portugal's second tier, so those resolve by name only and stay fictional unless a names file fills them. `applyRosterFile` realigns each slot's shuffled `academyBase` (`realignAcademyBases`, 2026-08-10): imported clubs are sorted by `computeTeamRating` and handed *that same group's* existing anchors in descending order, so a superclub can't inherit a weak youth anchor and drift over a long dynasty. **It's a permutation, not a recalculation** — same multiset, reassigned once, nothing derived from the imported ratings — so the anti-inflation equilibrium is untouched (a test pins the multiset). Only squad-*replaced* clubs take part, so a partial import can't degrade untouched clubs; the corollary is an imported club can only take an anchor another imported club held.

**Imported clubs draw no crest art (2026-08-10, user call).** `ClubCrest` keys crest images by **tid — a slot** — which is right for the shipped fictional clubs (a club's name is editable, its tid never changes) and wrong the moment a slot becomes a real club, since the badge then belongs to the club it displaced. `applyRosterFile` flags every slot the file *named* (squads or not — an identity-only entry is exactly the stale-crest case) with `StoredTeam.importedIdentity`, and `CrestArtProvider` suppresses art for those tids. **A context, not a prop**, because `ClubCrest` is rendered from 14 call sites that mostly hold only a tid; one of them forgetting the flag would show a wrong badge on that surface alone — the kind of bug that hides. Wrapped around the in-league shell (`Layout.tsx`) *and* the new-league club picker, so the preview matches the save. Optional field → no migration (absent = not imported). Knock-on: colors become the club's entire identity, so `mergeRosterFiles` now adopts real colors from the names file (the converter hashes them out of the club name — Liverpool came out purple).

**Custom club logos: a logo pack, matched by NAME rather than by slot (`core/teams/logoPack.ts`, `ui/logoImages.ts`, 2026-09-03, user ask).** The bullet above is the problem statement: the game ships 80 crests covering England's and Spain's top two divisions, so 546 of 626 clubs wear a swatch, and a roster import makes it *worse* by design — the slot's badge is suppressed and nothing replaces it. A pack is a sibling of the roster file (`{format: "world-soccer-sim-logos", formatVersion: 1, logos: [{match, image}]}`, `image` a data URL) loaded from the same New League screen, and the picker also takes **loose picture files**, folding them into one pack with the filename as the club name — because the thing a person actually has is a folder of PNGs, and making them build a JSON first would put a build step in front of the feature.
- **The two files match on different things and that is the load-bearing distinction.** A roster file maps clubs **positionally** onto a competition's slots, which it must: the clubs it carries have no identity in the save yet, they *are* the identity being installed. A logo pack matches by **name**, because by the time it applies the names exist and a name is the only thing the author of a badge folder knows. Matching a logo positionally would mis-badge every club the moment a roster file listed its clubs in a different order. The corollary the UI has to state out loud: **load the roster file first**, or the badges go looking for clubs that don't exist yet.
- **Stored in its own IndexedDB store (`crests`, keyed `[lid, tid]`, DB_VERSION 4 → 5), never on `LeagueStore`.** Same argument as `players`/`retirees`/`careers` rather than a new one: the league record is rewritten in full on every mutation and `structuredClone`d to the worker on every sim, so a few MB of badges there would be paid for on every lineup drag and every matchday — the exact bug versions 2, 3 and 4 exist to undo. Nothing in `src/core` knows crests exist; they take no rng draw, cross no worker boundary and are read by no sim code, so **no dynasty audit and no scoreline rebase**. `saveLeague` doesn't touch the store (badges are written once at import), `deleteLeague` clears it **inside its own transaction** so a reused lid can't inherit them, and `migrate.ts` needed nothing — a save written before this had no badges, and empty is the truth rather than a shape waiting to be split.
- **Images are downscaled on the way in and that is not optional.** `imageToLogoDataUrl` re-encodes to `CREST_IMAGE_SIZE` (160) square WebP at 0.9, never upscaling, on a transparent ground. Real badges run ~110 KB each (the size of the shipped art) and a 626-club world of them is ~69 MB read into memory and written to disk for something drawn at 20 pixels; re-encoded it is ~3 MB. Decoded through an `HTMLImageElement` rather than `createImageBitmap` **for SVG**, which many badge packs are and which `createImageBitmap` has never handled consistently — rasterizing is also what makes accepting SVG *safe*, since an SVG is a document that can carry script and none of it survives a canvas. `MAX_LOGO_DATA_URL` (512 KB) only ever catches a pack somebody hand-wrote around that step.
- **`isImageDataUrl` is a security check, not a validation nicety.** These strings go straight into an `<img src>`, and `data:` covers `text/html` and `image/svg+xml` as readily as PNG, so the parser accepts exactly the raster types a canvas emits. Enforced twice — in `parseLogoPack` and again in `exportImport`'s reader — because a save file is a thing people send each other.
- **A custom badge outranks BOTH the shipped art and the imported-club suppression**, which is the whole point: suppression exists to stop a real club wearing the fictional badge of the slot it took, and this is the answer to exactly that. `CustomCrestProvider` is a second context beside `CrestArtProvider` for the reason that one is a context at all — `ClubCrest` is rendered from 14 sites holding nothing but a tid. Wrapped around the in-league shell *and* the new-league picker, in the same order, so the preview matches the save. `objectFit: contain` because a custom badge is whatever shape the file was and every caller sizes its layout on `size`.
- **The export carries them as a SIBLING key, stripped before `migrateLeague`.** `migrateLeague` hands back the object it was given, so a `crests` key left on it would ride onto the league record and be re-serialised on every mutation from then on — silently, and only for saves that had been through a file. The reader is deliberately **lenient** where `parseLogoPack` is strict (a bad badge must never cost someone a 60-season dynasty), and `importLeagueJSON` now returns `{league, crests}` rather than a `LeagueStore` — a signature change taken over a wrapper precisely so a future caller can't silently drop the badges.
- **The AI prompt says there is NO logo field, and that line is written to head off a mistake rather than in response to one.** `colors` sits in the club shape as the club's visual identity, so a badge is the natural next key for an AI to invent, and `parseRosterFile` builds its output from named fields — an invented one is **dropped in silence**. That is this feature's own failure arriving from a new direction: the author believes the badges are in the file, gets none, and has nothing to tell them why. Deliberately a *don't* plus a pointer at the real route (image files, same screen), never an instruction to produce one — asking a language model for a picture gets hallucinated base64, which *would* be fatal, since `parseLogoPack` rejects a data URL it cannot read. `rosterAiPrompt.test.ts` pins the wording, the pointer, the absence of any `data:image` string, **and** the silent-discard behaviour the claim rests on — so making the parser strict about unknown keys fails the test rather than leaving the prompt saying the opposite of what happens.
- **Creation-only, like the roster import, but for a much weaker reason — and it is the obvious next move.** A roster import is creation-only because it *destroys careers*; a badge destroys nothing, so the only thing keeping this at creation is that the picker lives there. An existing real-clubs save cannot be badged at all today, which is the likeliest thing a player will want. `saveCrests(lid, byTid)` + `resolveLogoPack(league.teams, pack)` is the whole of it wherever a second surface goes.

**Roster import is creation-only (2026-08-10, user call).** The per-save "Import Teams" button is gone; the entry point is Leagues → **Import Custom League** → `/new-league?roster=1`, and since 2026-08-28 a "Load roster files" button on plain `/new-league` as well. **Creation-time is the invariant, not which screen** — both land in the same page state. Reason: a roster file *replaces squads*, and `applyRosterFile` drops every replaced player who has no recorded stats — on a save in progress that silently deletes real careers, stat lines and the transfer history keyed to those pids. At creation there is nothing to destroy. Three consequences worth knowing: **(a)** `applyRosterFileToNewLeague` (`teams/rosterImport.ts`) is the entry point, and it re-runs `assignAIFormations` + `reconcileScoutingObserved` after the import, because `createLeagueState` derives both from squads the import then throws away (formations picked for deleted players; the user's fog-of-war stamps pruned along with the pids they pointed at, leaving the imported squad permanently unscouted). It is **only** safe on a fresh league — re-stamping scouting resets every observation to season 1, which mid-save hands the user a fully-scouted squad. **(b)** `resolveRosterSlots` now takes a structural `RosterSlotWorld` (`{competitions, teams}`) rather than a `LeagueStore`, so the club picker can resolve a file against `worldTeamSlots()` — the slot layout of a save that doesn't exist yet — and show real club names *before* paying 4s to generate 6000 players. `worldTeamSlots` duplicates `generateWorld`'s tid layout by necessity; `rosterImport.test.ts` pins it against a real generated world so the two can't drift. **(c)** Crest art does **not** follow an import (`ClubCrest` keys images by tid, i.e. by slot), so an imported club wears whatever crest its slot had. Colors do follow.

**The AI prompt has to live on the screens that IMPORT, and for a year it didn't (2026-09-02).** `buildImportPromptText` (`teams/rosterAiPrompt.ts`) was offered only from `TopBar`, `TopBar` renders only inside `Layout`, and `/leagues` and `/new-league` both sit **outside** `Layout` (`App.tsx`) — while import is creation-only per the entry above. So the only copy of the format spec lived behind a save you had to already have, on a route you had to already have left; the roster screen's own help text said as much, telling the reader to go and find "the top bar of any save". **The tell that this mattered is what arrives in the wild**: a 232-club / 5,873-player all-time-teams file (2026-09-02) broke four rules the prompt states plainly — no `country`/`tier` on any entry, real-world division sizes rather than this world's (French 20 against 18, Scottish 17 against 12), an invented competition ("Brazilian Division 1"), and adjectival names *reconstructed* rather than copied ("Netherlands Division 1" for "Dutch Division 1"). That is what a file written without the spec looks like, and none of it is the author's fault. Fixed by widening the builder from `LeagueStore` to the structural **`RosterSlotWorld`** it already had lying around — a save satisfies it, and so does `worldTeamSlots()`, so a pre-save screen can describe a world that doesn't exist yet. `CopyAiPromptButton` is the one control (clipboard, download fallback, transient label); `/new-league` passes the **live configured** world so a renamed or added league is in the prompt, `/leagues` passes the shipped default because it has no world of its own. `test/ui/aiPromptReach.test.tsx` pins all three routes and **matches a `<button>` element, not the label string** — the old help text contained that same label, so a substring check passes on the broken code.
- **The `colors` trap is the one FATAL authoring mistake, and it is caused by ACCURACY.** `parseRosterFile` demands exactly two hex strings and *throws*, and a throw rejects the entire file — so one club costs an author every other one. That is what killed the file above: Fluminense and Bahia listed their real **three** colours. The prompt now states the two-colour rule, names it as the commonest rejection, and carries it on the pre-answer checklist. **It also had to qualify its own framing**: the three numbered list rules genuinely "fail QUIETLY", and the prompt said so unscoped, which teaches an AI that everything degrades gracefully and nothing is worth re-reading. Field *shapes* are the opposite and now say so. `rosterAiPrompt.test.ts` pins the wording **and** the behaviour behind it (a three-colour club throws; removing that one club accepts the same file), so making the parser lenient here fails the test rather than leaving the prompt warning about something that no longer happens.

**Roster import takes several files (2026-08-11).** A world is authored a league at a time (an AI won't emit twelve competitions of squads in one answer), so both pickers — Leagues → Import and the `?roster=1` screen's "Add another file" — are `multiple` and the selected files are folded by `combineRosterFiles` (`teams/rosterFile.ts`) into the single file `applyRosterFileToNewLeague` applies. Competitions are independent, so combining is concatenation in load order; the one ambiguous case, two files claiming the same competition, can't be interleaved (clubs map *positionally* onto slots) so the later file wins outright and the collision is reported. `PendingRoster` therefore hands over `files[]`, and `NewLeague` keeps the loaded `sources` so later picks add to them rather than replace. A file that won't parse is skipped and named on the picker screen; on the Leagues screen it stops the batch instead, because that screen is left behind on the way to the picker and any error it raised would go with it.

The first `npm test` after any `src/core` change rebuilds the cached test worlds (see `test/` below); later runs reuse them. `SOCCER_GM_NO_FIXTURE_CACHE=1 npm test` skips the cache.

**A local `npm test` runs on half the cores, deliberately (`localMaxWorkers` in `vite.config.ts`, 2026-08-29).** Vitest defaults to about one worker per core and on this suite that is measurably too many: the work is sustained full-world multi-season sims and the machines are fanless laptops that throttle under exactly that. Measured on an 8-core Air, **6 workers over 6 heavy files took 2025s while the *sum* of per-test times hit 8476s against ~1038s uncontended — an ~8x inflation** — so past a low worker count this suite scales *negatively*: more workers make the wall-clock worse, not merely sub-linear. The same effect from the other side: capping workers was worth **1.96x on the full suite** (1117s → 570s) in the two-machine runner. **RAM was NOT the mechanism when this shipped and IS one now, and the flip is worth knowing about — the first full run after third divisions (#308) exhausted memory and took the host down (2026-09-02).** The original reading was right at the time: peak resident size was ~445MB per worker, so nine fit comfortably; the cost was memory bandwidth and thermals. Going from 420 clubs / 7,092 fixtures to **626 / 10,538** invalidated it. Measured in one process on a 16GB machine, 2-division → 3-division: `league.played` **129MB → 192MB** of JSON (1.49x, tracking the world) but **peak heap for a simmed season 471MB → 1082MB (2.30x)** — *superlinear*, because a matchday's box scores are all live at once. Nothing accumulates across seasons (the offseason clears `played` and live heap drops back to ~100MB) but **resident size never comes back**: across three seasons RSS ratcheted 1205 → 1393 → 1510MB against a ~100MB live heap, since V8 does not return freed pages to the OS. So a worker on a heavy file costs **~2-3GB and keeps it**, and locally the sequencer inherits vitest's slowest-first `sort()` (`shard()` is a no-op without `--shard`), so a run *starts* by putting the heaviest sim files on every worker at once — the worst case by construction. The cap is therefore now the **lower of a core limit and a memory limit** (`WORKER_PEAK_GB` 3, `HOST_RESERVE_GB` 4): 16GB/10-core lands on 4 workers, 8GB on 1. A per-worker `--max-old-space-size` ceiling is set from the same `WORKER_PEAK_GB`, so `maxWorkers × budget` is a real bound rather than an assumption — **Node's ~4.3GB default bounds one worker without bounding the run**, which is exactly how the machine died with no test reporting anything. Verified after the change: the six heaviest files together peak at **6.5GB** and the full suite at ****6.8GB** (202 files / 2093 tests green in 26.6 min, 4 workers)**. The thermal half of the story is unchanged and still applies — it *worsens as the machine heats*, with the same three files taking ~506s cool and 988s after six hours of back-to-back sims (the `shardPartition.ts` "234s and 557s an hour apart" warning, one level up). Half the cores is deliberately conservative rather than tuned — the true optimum is machine- and temperature-dependent and could not be pinned through that much drift. `VITEST_MAX_WORKERS=n` overrides (and throws on a non-positive value rather than silently ignoring it). **CI is deliberately untouched**: its runners are already core-poor, they are not thermally-limited laptops, and CI gates merges, so there is no upside and real downside if the guess is wrong for that hardware.

## Analytics & deploy config

**PostHog needs `VITE_POSTHOG_KEY`/`VITE_POSTHOG_HOST` at BUILD time, and a build without them fails silently (2026-08-20).** Vite inlines `import.meta.env.VITE_*`, so the values are baked into the bundle by `npm run build` — they are not runtime config, and a host-side secret cannot reach an assets-only static deploy. Missing, `posthog.init()` (`src/ui/main.tsx`) is handed `undefined`: nothing throws, nothing logs, and the site reports no pageviews, no gameplay events and no crashes while looking perfectly healthy. That is what the 2026-08-15 Vercel → Cloudflare move (`wrangler.jsonc`, assets-only) did — the vars lived in the Vercel project, didn't come along, and production went analytics-dark for five days while Vercel *preview* deploys kept reporting normally, which is what made it look like a PostHog outage rather than a build problem. **The values are therefore committed, per build target** — `.env.production` (web) and `.env.itch`, both carrying the key, so any checkout builds a working bundle with no dashboard configuration anywhere and there is nothing left to lose in the next host migration. Safe because the project key is PostHog's public write-only token, already served to every visitor in the bundle. Three gotchas: **(a)** Vite loads only `.env` and `.env.<mode>`, and `.env.production` is itself a mode file, so it is *not* loaded by `--mode itch`/`--mode crazygames` — hence the duplication rather than one shared file (`.env` would cover every mode, but it is gitignored, which is the out-of-band storage this arrangement exists to avoid). **(b)** `.env.crazygames` deliberately carries **no** key: that build aliases `posthog-js` to a no-op shim (`vite.config.ts` → `src/ui/vendor/posthogNoop.ts`), so the omission looks like an oversight and isn't. **(c)** `npm run dev` runs mode "development" and so loads neither, meaning dev sessions report nothing by default — which is wanted, since dev clicks would otherwise land in the same project as real players and burn quota. `.env.example` exists only to opt a local dev session in. **Verify a deploy, don't assume:** `curl -s https://worldsoccersim.org/assets/index-*.js | grep -c phc_` — zero means that build has no key.

**worldsoccersim.org DEPLOYS ITSELF, and the repo gives no sign of it — this cost an hour and nearly shipped a duplicate pipeline (2026-09-04, `docs/cloudflare.md`).** Cloudflare **Workers Builds** is connected to the repo and publishes on every push to main. Nothing in `.github/workflows/` does it, and all the repo contains is `wrangler.jsonc` sitting alone, which reads exactly like a deploy someone runs by hand — that inference was drawn **twice in one session, confidently, and was wrong both times**. **It surfaces as a CHECK RUN, not a GitHub deployment**, which is the specific trap: querying `/deployments` shows only `vercel[bot]` and `github-pages` and looks like proof nothing automates Cloudflare. Look at `gh api repos/.../commits/<sha>/check-runs` for `app.slug == "cloudflare-workers-and-pages"`.
  - **The behaviour to know is LAG, not failure.** Measured merge-to-live: #331 **6m21s**, #329 ~19 min, #316 ~20 min — single digits usually, ~20 when Cloudflare's dependency cache is cold. So the domain is legitimately behind main for a few minutes after every merge, and checking inside that window presents as *"I merged this feature and it isn't in the game"*, which points at the feature rather than the clock. Two non-failures: **rapid merges are coalesced** (#318 and #329 merged two minutes apart and only #329 built — an *absent* check run means a later commit superseded it), and **nothing gates the deploy on tests** (PR runs gate merges; main publishes on push regardless).
  - **The Vercel project is a leftover of the pre-2026-08-15 host and the domain is NOT pointed at it** (`server: cloudflare`, no `x-vercel-*` on any response) — yet it still builds every push including a `Production` deployment, so a current Vercel link proves the code is on main and proves nothing about the live site. That is how this presented: feature on the Vercel link, not on the domain, the two assumed to be one thing.
  - **A second deploy workflow was built and CLOSED unmerged** (#333). Running `wrangler deploy` from Actions duplicates what Workers Builds already does, so it would mean two pipelines racing to publish the same Worker on every push; it also would not have helped, since `npm ci` dominates and Cloudflare did #331 end to end in 6m21s. The only real reason to own the deploy is **gating it on CI green** — and that is a trade, not an upgrade (~6 min becomes ~35), and it requires turning Cloudflare's build **off** first. One deployer or the other, never both.
  - **How to date a stale deploy**, since guessing from feature greps is what produced the wrong answer: `git archive <sha> | tar -x`, symlink `node_modules`, `npx vite build`, compare the `index-*.js` hash **and byte size** to the live one — Vite output is deterministic given the same lockfile (#316 → `index-BSYiCOl2.js`, 1,748,391 bytes, matching exactly). **Do NOT date a bundle by grepping changelog text**: `changelog/index.ts` globs its entries and Vite code-splits them into `Changelog-*.js`, so every changelog string reads 0 in the index chunk whatever the commit — a decisive-looking negative that is pure noise.
  - **The two guards that DID ship** cover what the deploy doesn't check. `ci.yml`'s typecheck job now runs `npm run build` and asserts `phc_` in `dist/assets` — the silent-analytics failure the paragraph above documents, moved from five days to one PR (added to that job rather than a new one because the file's own header is about a 20-job account ceiling). `verify-live-site.yml` fires on Cloudflare's check run and **deploys nothing**: red if the build failed (otherwise a grey check that scrolls away while the site sits on the previous bundle), else asserts the domain serves a bundle with its key and a working SPA fallback. It cannot run on the PR that adds it — GitHub dispatches `check_run` only against workflows already on the default branch.
  - **The generalizable lesson, and the reason this entry is long: the deploy pipeline lived somewhere the repo could not see, and absence of evidence in one API was twice read as evidence of absence.** Both times the correction came from the user saying the explanation did not match their experience. When production and a preview disagree, suspect the deploy — then *measure how far behind and by what mechanism* before narrating why.

**The free tier is 1M events/month, and it has been exhausted once.** Autocapture fired on every click in a click-heavy game (685k events in 30 days, 67% of volume) and blew the allowance on 2026-08-03; ingestion stopped project-wide for the rest of the cycle and only `$exception` kept flowing, on its own quota. `autocapture: false` shipped in #205. Nothing reads autocapture data — every dashboard is built on the explicit events in `src/ui/analytics.ts`, whose rule is "a handful of meaningful moments, not one event per click". Keep it that way before adding events.

## Git workflow

- Keep work committed: after code changes, commit locally with a clear message rather than leaving the tree dirty.
- Keep the remote in sync: push to `origin` after committing so local `main` and GitHub `main` never drift.
- A merged PR isn't done until it's pulled into local `main` (`git checkout main && git pull`).
- Only skip this if the user explicitly asks you to hold off.

## Shared-file conventions (committed files are the only durable record)

**One account (proelium27) works this repo.** It used to be two (joeltmeyer/joeltm82, last commit 2026-07-12, 17 of ~580 commits), and these conventions were written for that — but they are kept because the reason survives the second account: **a Claude session's memory is private to that session and dies with it, so committed files are the only thing the next session can read.** The same goes for concurrent work by one person — several branches and worktrees are normally open at once here — so "don't make two branches fight over one file" is still a live constraint, not a historical one. Three files must be kept in sync in the *same PR* as any change they cover:

1. **This file's "Milestone status"** — update when a milestone advances/reorders or a major architectural/design decision is made. Routine typo/UI/single-bug fixes don't need it. If you spot drift between this file and `main`, fix it as part of your work. Unsure if a change is milestone-significant? Ask.
2. **The in-game Manual** (`src/ui/pages/Manual.tsx`, `/manual`) — player-facing feature ledger, BBGM-manual style (single scrollable page, second person, plain-spoken, concrete numbers quoted from `src/core/constants.ts`). Update the relevant section when a **player-visible** feature ships/changes/is removed, or when a quoted constant is retuned (grep for the old value). Explains hidden mechanics' *behavior* without spoiling hidden *values*; keep the FAQ honest about known gaps.
3. **The player-facing Changelog** (`/changelog`; `src/ui/pages/Changelog.tsx` renders `CHANGELOG` from `src/core/changelog/index.ts`, which **globs** `src/core/changelog/entries/*.ts`) — ADD a new file `entries/YYYY-MM-DD-NN-slug.ts` default-exporting one dated, plain-language entry for any player-visible change; copy the newest existing file for the shape and voice. **Never edit a shared list to do this** — one-entry-per-file exists precisely so two open PRs can both ship a changelog entry without conflicting (still routine with one developer and several branches in flight), and reintroducing any central index (or a hand-ordered array) brings that conflict straight back. Order is derived: date descending, then filename. Manual = *how it works*; Changelog = *what changed and when*. Update both.

## Architecture (where things live)

- `src/core/` — pure sim logic (no UI/DOM). `constants.ts` is the central tuning table. Sub-areas: `players/` (generation, progression, youth, nationalities), `teams/`, `league/`, `ai/` (GM evaluation core), `transfers/`, `finance/`, `lineup/`, `cup/`, `scouting/`, plus top-level `offseason.ts`, `simThrough.ts`, `promotion.ts`, `loans.ts`, `awards.ts`, `godMode.ts`, `competitions.ts`.
- `src/engine/` — match sim (`matchSim.ts`, `attribution.ts`, `matchRating.ts`, its own `constants.ts`).
- `src/db/` — IndexedDB persistence (`leagueDb.ts`, `activeLeague.ts`) and **`migrate.ts`** (backfills every new field for old saves — update it whenever you add a persisted field).
- `src/ui/` — React pages (`pages/`), components, `context/LeagueContext.tsx` (all league-mutating actions, serialized through one promise chain).
- **Build targets** (`vite.config.ts`, `.env.<mode>`) — the default build serves worldsoccersim.org; `itch` and `crazygames` are **embedded** targets (someone else's iframe, at a path we don't control), grouped by the `EMBEDDED` set: relative `base` and hash routing, because there's no server to answer a history-API deep link. `crazygames` additionally strips AdSense and the SEO block from `index.html`, aliases `posthog-js` to a no-op, skips `<RouteSeo />` (or `useRouteSeo` recreates the canonical tag at runtime, pointing at a playable copy of the game — the one outbound link their rules forbid), and adds the CrazyGames SDK. **`src/ui/crazygames.ts` is the only place that touches the SDK**: no-ops everywhere else, queues every call behind the async `init()`, and survives the script being blocked. It also drops the announcement banner (theirs is a fixed 16:9 container as small as **914x514**, where a 36px strip is 7% of the height, and a full-width "Join the Discord" bar risks reading as the main CTA their rules disallow; the sidebar link is fine). **Two layout fixes it surfaced are global, not embed-only**, because they were equally broken in any narrow browser window: the top bar's inline save controls collapse into the existing overflow menu below `xl` (1200px) rather than `md` (768px) — the row needs ~1020px and was wrapping onto two lines and truncating the season — and the sidebar narrows 220px → 170px between 768px and 1199.98px. Full runbook + upload-form answers: `docs/crazygames.md`. Three gotchas: **(a)** the HTML surgery keys off marker comments and **throws if they go missing**, so editing `<head>` can't silently ship AdSense to review; **(b)** files in `public/` are copied verbatim and do *not* get Vite's base rewriting, so they must go through `publicAsset()`/`LOGO_URL` (`src/ui/publicAsset.ts`) — a bare `/favicon.png` resolved against the *host's* domain and 404'd the top-bar logo in both embedded builds, a bug that only shows up in an iframe at a subpath; **(c)** `dist-crazygames-harness.html` (repo root, never shipped) loads the built bundle in an iframe pinned to both measured CrazyGames sizes — the only way to see either the subpath bugs or the cramped layout, since a plain tab is neither iframed nor 16:9.
  - **`pages` is a fourth target and it is NOT embedded (2026-08-26).** It publishes the same game to `proelium27.github.io/world-soccer-sim/` from `.github/workflows/pages.yml` on every push to main. Not in `EMBEDDED`, deliberately: it is an ordinary page on its own domain, so it keeps history-API URLs rather than switching to hash routing. What it needs instead is the **two things a subpath host takes**, and both are silent when missing. **(a) A base path**, `PAGES_BASE` (default `/world-soccer-sim/`, which must track the repo's current name; the workflow fills it from `configure-pages`' reported base path, which is the only source that knows about a custom domain, and falls back to the repo name so a fork or rename needs no edit), which reaches the app as `import.meta.env.BASE_URL` and is now the **router's `basename`** — without it the browser sits at `/world-soccer-sim/manual` while React Router looks for a `/world-soccer-sim/manual` route and matches nothing. `basename` is `undefined` for the hash builds, whose base is the relative `./` (not a URL path, and a hash router has no prefix to strip). **(b) `404.html`**, a copy of `index.html` written by the pages build: Pages has no rewrite rules, and its one hook is serving that document for any path it can't resolve, with the requested URL intact — so a deep link boots the app on the right route. It answers **404**, which is invisible to a player and costs nothing in search because `SITE_ORIGIN` canonicalizes every page to worldsoccersim.org. The workflow asserts the file exists, since a missing fallback only shows up when someone opens a page other than the entry point. The pre-React placeholder's absolute `/leagues` links get the base prefix here for the same reason the CrazyGames build hash-rewrites them. **`.env.pages` carries the PostHog key** — a third copy, for the reason `.env.itch` spells out: `.env.production` is itself a mode file and is not read for `--mode pages`, so a key kept only there ships a bundle with `posthog.init(undefined)`, silent and inert. **The base is baked into every asset URL at build time, so RENAMING THE REPO BREAKS THE LIVE SITE until it is redeployed** — the deployed HTML keeps asking for the old name's `/assets/`, the page loads and every asset 404s. Measured, not hypothetical: `soccer-gm` became `world-soccer-sim` hours after the first deploy and the site went blank. No static file can detect its own URL changed, so a rebuild is the only fix and also the whole fix (the workflow reads the name at build time). Same applies to adding or removing a custom domain.
- `test/` — vitest. `test/validation/` holds the M1/M4 §8 gate tests.
  - **A world-wide `max` is not a league statistic, and the M3 top-scorer gate learned this the hard way (2026-08-11).** It asserted a Golden-Boot-shaped band (18–36) but measured the max goals over **every player in the world, second divisions included**. Both halves drift for reasons unrelated to scoring realism: a max rises with sample size (12 competitions → 16 raised it on its own), and tier-2 defences are weaker, so the world max was *set by a tier-2 player* and exceeded every top-flight leader that season (43 vs 32, 40 vs 34). It failed at a 36.2 mean while all 40 measured tier-1 Golden Boots sat between 23 and 34 — inside the band, never once above it. Now measures the **mean tier-1 top scorer** (27.7, per-season range 27.0–28.8), which is what the band describes and is invariant to both world size and tier-2 quirks. `scripts/topScorerProbe.ts` prints both side by side. **Widening the ceiling would have been the wrong fix** — it keeps a number that needs re-widening every time a country is added.
  - **The M1 mismatch gate had the same fault, third time in this file (2026-08-27).** "Strong home beats weak 70-80%" measured **one** league seed's strongest-vs-weakest pair. How far apart that pair lands is itself a draw — the OVR gap runs 16.9 to 18.8 across seeds — so the reading carries **±3.3pp of spread against a 10pp band**. Measured on **untouched `origin/main`**: `73.60 / 74.42 / 76.07 / 78.65 / 78.75 / 79.88 / 76.89 / 83.90` (mean 77.77, sd 3.30), i.e. **seed 71 already sat outside the band on main** and seed 1, the one the gate read, was passing by 0.12pp. A change worth **+0.23pp on the mean** then failed it. Now averages the same `LEAGUE_SEEDS` the mid-vs-mid gate already uses, band untouched, mean 78.0. **The recurring lesson: when a §8 gate fails, first measure its estimator across seeds on main — twice now the gate was reading one arbitrary sample, and both times widening the band would have hidden that.**
  - **"Average team" must mean the average team, not the middle array index — the M1 §8 benchmark gate (2026-08-13).** `averageMidVsMid` sampled one club per seed as `seedComps[len / 2]`. Strength targets are shuffled across tids at generation, so that is an arbitrary club, not the median one — the same file already sorts by `avgOvr` "rather than array position" to pick its strong/weak pair, and this function was simply never given the same treatment. The gate therefore reported the sampled club rather than the league, and it sat on its floor (goals/game **2.6019** against a 2.6 floor). A change that provably did not move scoring — **2.8566 → 2.8560 measured across all 100 teams, a difference of 0.0006** — failed it, purely because a different club landed at that index. It now averages **every** team's self-match, which lands all six metrics inside their **existing** bands on both the old commit and the change that exposed it. **No band was moved**, deliberately: the bands are the spec's realism targets and none of them was the problem. `scripts/midVsMidProbe.ts` prints both estimators side by side. Same lesson as the top-scorer gate above — fix the statistic, don't widen the band.
  - **A 5-season average is not precise enough to gate on — the M1 table-spread gate (2026-08-16).** It averaged **5** seasons of champion points against a 78-94 band. A single season's champion total has sd ≈ **5** points (`scripts/championPointsProbe.ts`, 20 seasons span 72-91), so that average carried ~**2.2 points of its own sampling error**, against a true mean of ~82 sitting only ~1.8 of those above the floor — i.e. **it could fail on seed luck with nothing wrong with the sim**, which is the precise sense in which CLAUDE.md already said it "sits near its floor by construction". Demonstrated: raising `YELLOW_GIVEN_FOUL` 0.11 → 0.18 read **75.8** here, a clean failure, while the *same change* over 20 seasons moved the champion mean **up** (81.75 → 82.20) and **reduced** its spread (sd 5.80 → 4.66). Now averages **20** seasons (~22s, against 13-minute gates elsewhere), which halves the estimator's error; **the band was deliberately not moved**, and the widened gate was verified green on **both** sides of the change so the fix can't be tuned to it. Third instance of the same lesson as the top-scorer and "average team" gates: **fix the statistic, don't widen the band** — and note the failing statistic here was the *sample size*, not the quantity.
  - **A minimum over 320 clubs is not a safety invariant either — the offseason roster-floor test (2026-08-13).** It asserted every club sits at or above `ROSTER_SAFETY_FLOOR` (18) after one offseason. Nothing enforces that: retirement and contract expiry are not sales, so `runAITransferMarket`'s per-sale depth floor never sees them, and `ensureUserRosterSafety` can only promote academy players a club actually has. Measured over five seasons on the test's own seed, **`origin/main` itself dips to 16 — the user's own club, season 4** — and the test only ever looked at season 1, where it happened to land exactly on 18. It was passing by luck, and any change that perturbs match results can tip it (across 8 seeds at season 1: main `20 19 18 19 18 18 18 20`, i.e. sitting *on* the floor half the time). Now asserts the engine's real requirement as a hard floor (**11** fit players, below which `selectXI` silently leaves slots empty) plus the healthy-squad target on the **5th percentile**, which is 21 on every seed on both sides and doesn't move. Dips recover by the next offseason. If the floor should be a genuine guarantee for AI clubs, that needs a mechanism (AI free agency refilling to it), not a tighter assertion — see the lower-severity leftovers about squads dropping below 11.
- **CI shards are packed by cost, not by file count (`test/helpers/shardPartition.ts`, 2026-08-25).** Vitest's `--shard` hashes each path with sha1, sorts, and slices into equal *counts*, which is deterministic and cost-blind — a bad pair here, because the suite's work sits almost entirely in a few full-world sim files. Measured over three consecutive CI runs: slowest shard 1749/1765/1863s against a fastest of 40/41/47s, on ~75 minutes of total work, i.e. one runner carried the build while another idled for half an hour, the same way every run. A `CostAwareSequencer` (wired at `test.sequence.sequencer` in `vite.config.ts`) weights each file and greedily packs the shards instead. Three things to know: **(a)** the weights are a hand-maintained table of measured seconds, defaulting to 10s for anything unlisted — being wrong costs *balance only*, never correctness, since `partitionByCost` is total and disjoint whatever the weights say (`shardPartition.test.ts` pins that, and it is the point of the file: a partition bug drops a file from every shard and CI still reports green). Add an entry when a new file runs longer than ~30s. **(b)** **Balancing alone did NOT speed CI up, and the measurement is the point.** The floor is the slowest single *file*: on the first packed run shard 1 ran `offseason.test.ts` **alone** for 1933s, against a pre-balancing worst shard of 1749-1863s — i.e. the old worst shard was already essentially that one file, so there was never a wall-clock win to take (the estimate that there was assumed no single file exceeded an even share; this one is ~2.2x it). What balancing bought is the other five shards getting lighter — spare capacity, not a faster build. It also makes the floor *mechanical*: shard 1 is provably one file, so the next move is unambiguous. **(c)** **The giants have since been split, and that is what actually moved the number.** `offseason.test.ts` (23 tests, ~1933s CI) became `offseason` / `offseasonSquads` / `offseasonRetirement` / `offseasonFinance` / `offseasonSolvency`, and `international.test.ts` (25 tests, ~1082s) became `international` (the pure structural tests, which drop to ~7s once the sims leave) / `internationalCampaign` / `internationalPlayerRecord` / `internationalEquivalence` / `internationalConfederationCups`. Every test moved **byte-identically** — they were already independent, each building its own seeded rng — with the shared `playFullSeason` / `advance` helpers lifted into `test/helpers/offseasonLeague.ts` and `test/helpers/intlLeague.ts`. The packing is what then spreads the pieces; without it two halves land back on one shard. **Measured after the split: shards 603/639/666/685/698/725s against a 669s even share, i.e. within 8% of the best any partition could do, and CI wall-clock 1863s -> 725s (~31 min -> ~12 min).** Total work did not grow (4016s against a 4131-4922s baseline), so splitting a hot sim file across processes costs nothing measurable in re-JIT. **(d)** With no file dominating, the binding constraint is now **total work / shard count**, so the next lever is the shard count in `ci.yml` — but a shard is **not free**, and that is new (2026-08-29). GitHub Free allows **20 concurrent jobs per account**, so shards compete for slots across every branch running at once: at six shards, three active branches want 42 jobs and the repo sits pinned at exactly 20 in progress with the rest queued, which looks like a run creeping through its shards one at a time. Raising the count past what the ceiling can serve buys nothing and slows every *other* branch. The first thing to fix when CI feels slow is therefore demand, not the split: `ci.yml`'s `push` trigger is filtered to main precisely because an unfiltered `push` alongside `pull_request` ran the whole suite **twice on the same commit** (verified by run ids with `event=push` and `event=pull_request` on one sha), doubling demand for nothing. The same file also carries a `concurrency` group so a new push **supersedes** the branch's in-flight run rather than queueing behind it — the other half of that backlog was stale runs finishing six shards nobody would read. It is deliberately **not** applied to main (`cancel-in-progress` is conditioned on the ref): merging two PRs in quick succession would otherwise cancel the first commit's run and leave it permanently unverified, not more splitting — the packing test asserts against exactly that bound (`max(slowest file, total/shards)`) rather than a fixed number, so it stays honest in either regime. **(e)** CI runners are ~1.5x slower than a dev machine here, and a dev machine running full-world sims back to back **thermally throttles** — the same file measured 234s and 557s an hour apart — so treat the weights as an ordering, not as seconds, and don't chase a small discrepancy. **(f) A WORLD-SIZE CHANGE INVALIDATES THE WHOLE TABLE, and not in a way that still sorts correctly (2026-09-02).** The 626-club world (#308) grew total CI work **5615s → 8367s (+49%, tracking the club count almost exactly)**, but it grew it *unevenly*: a file that builds a world and sims seasons roughly tripled while a pure-derivation file did not move, so the packing — which only cares about relative cost — went genuinely wrong rather than merely scaling. Measured on run 33580546751: shards **870/1184/1306/1538/1558/1911s**, i.e. the slowest at **1.37x** the 1394s even share, while the model believed it had balanced them to within 1% (it predicted 1000-1009s each). **Fourteen files that had crossed the ~30s bar were still on the 10s default**, `cupIntegration.test.ts` at 743s of CI time against a weight of 10. The table was rebuilt from that one run's per-file `tests` durations ÷1.5 (its stated local-seconds basis), taking every number from **one** contended run so they are consistent with each other, where the old table mixed months and worlds; re-scored against the same measurements that lands **3105/3073/3138/3149/3117/3149 — max/even 1.01**. Refresh it the same way after the next world change, from a green run rather than file by file: `gh run view <id> --log | grep -oE '\S+\.test\.tsx? \([0-9]+ tests?\) [0-9]+ms'`. **But the wall clock did NOT follow, and that predicted 1.01 is the trap.** Measured after the refresh (run 33641959090): shards **1245/1304/1438/1531/1501/1802s**, so the slowest went 1911s → 1802s and CI ~32 → ~30 min, not the ~24 predicted. The *work* did even out (summed test-time 1.13x the even share, against 1.37x before) — **but summed test-time is not shard wall clock, and this table can only balance the former.** Two causes, both inside that single run: **effective parallelism varied 1.72–2.48x across the six runners** (shard 2 drew the slowest and finished last on the second-*lightest* load, which no packing can fix), and **per-file cost swings run to run** (`offseasonRetirement` 1359s → 1481s, `internationalConfederationCups` 813s → 574s — ordering survives, which is all packing needs). So refresh after a world change, but don't expect proportional wall-clock gains and never tune these against one run's wall clock. **The corollary is that (d) has expired:** the slowest single file (`offseasonRetirement.test.ts`, 1359-1481s CI) now sits *inside* the range the shards actually finish in, so a seventh shard buys nothing and the lever is splitting that file again, not adding runners.
- **A local run can be split across two Macs (`npm run test:cluster`, `scripts/testCluster.sh`, 2026-08-28).** Same idea as the CI sharding above, pointed at hardware you own: it probes the other machine, rsyncs the working tree (so uncommitted work is tested), runs shard 1/2 here and 2/2 there in parallel, and merges the blob reports into one pass/fail. Unreachable machine (asleep, offline, no key) falls back to a normal local run after a 2s timeout; `-- --local` forces it. Config is `.test-cluster.json` (gitignored — it names *your* machine), seeded from `.test-cluster.example.json`. The split reuses `partitionByCost` with an optional `capacities` array, because two laptops are not two identical CI runners; **with uniform capacities it reduces to exactly the old least-loaded rule, so CI's split is byte-for-byte unchanged and a test pins that.** Gotchas, all found by running it against a real second machine rather than by reasoning: **(a) A file timed ALONE is not the number it costs during a full run, and the gap is large.** Measured on an 8GB MacBook, `offseasonFinance.test.ts` is 156s solo and 244s alongside three other heavy files — 1.57x at only *four* workers on *eight* cores, so it is memory bandwidth and thermals, not CPU contention; parallel efficiency across those four was ~55%. The `FILE_WEIGHTS_SECONDS` table holds full-run numbers, so a solo `npx vitest run <file>` reading far lower is expected — **do not paste it in**, since mixing a solo number into a table of contended ones reorders the packing on a measurement artefact. This is the same effect as the thermal caveat in (e) above, one level up. **(b) Hence a second machine is worth more than its core count**: it brings an independent memory subsystem and thermal budget, which is the part a small fanless machine is short of, and why the split is capacity-weighted at all. **(c) ~~Do NOT split more test files for this.~~ TRUE UNTIL 2026-09-02, FALSE NOW.** After the #278 splits the heaviest file was ~156s solo, well under a two-machine even share, so files had stopped being the ceiling and further splitting bought nothing but churn. The 626-club world undid that: `offseasonRetirement.test.ts` alone is ~1359s on CI (~900s local), which is ~3% under the six-shard wall-clock floor and a large fraction of a two-machine share. It is the one file worth splitting again — see (f) one bullet up. **(d) Two failure modes here report GREEN while being wrong, so both fail loudly instead.** Every shard computes the whole partition and keeps its own slice, so the machines agree only while they agree on the capacities — a malformed `VITEST_SHARD_CAPACITIES` therefore *throws* rather than falling back to an equal split, which would silently run some files twice and others never. And the runner hashes `src`/`test`/`scripts`/config on both sides after rsync and refuses to run on a mismatch, because a remote passing against stale code is a green that means nothing. **(e) `ssh host cmd` gets a non-login shell**, which on macOS never sources `/etc/zprofile` and so never runs `path_helper`, leaving PATH without `/usr/local/bin` — `npm ci` fails with "command not found" on a machine where node works perfectly in person. Remote commands go through a login shell so neither machine needs its dotfiles edited. **(f) `vitest --merge-reports` needs `run`** (without it vitest defaults to watch mode and merging is rejected), and it reads **every** file in `.vitest-reports/` as a blob and dies on anything else — so per-machine logs and exit codes live in `.test-cluster-run/`, never beside the blobs. **(g) Autotune only fires on a full run**, when both sides ran long enough that rsync/`npm ci`/startup isn't most of the elapsed time, damped halfway with a 4:1 clamp: bins hold whole files so a single run always over- or undershoots, and an early filtered run moved the remote from 2.0 to 8.86, which is not a 8.9x machine. **(h) `set -o pipefail` does NOT cross the ssh boundary, and that cost a whole run (2026-09-03).** It is set at the top of the script, but `rsh` opens a fresh login zsh, which reports only the last stage of a pipeline — so `npm ci --silent 2>&1 | tail -2 || exit 1` took its status from `tail` and could never fail. A half-finished remote install reported success, the suite ran against a `node_modules` with no vite in it, `npx` quietly fetched a *different major version* of vitest, and both shards died at startup about 20 minutes in. The rsync excludes `node_modules`, so nothing else would have noticed either. Fixed by setting the option inside the remote shell. **The general shape: any `set -o` in this script is local-only; a remote command needs its own.** **(i) The verdict is the two SHARD EXIT CODES, not the merged report, and it produces false REDs as well as the false greens (d) warns about.** It has to read exit codes — a shard that dies halfway writes a partial blob and the merge would call the files it did run green — but the converse happened on 2026-09-03: a shard exited 1 with **every test in its own blob passing, no unhandled errors, and the same shard exiting 0 on an immediate re-run against the same tree**. A full 204-file / 2165-test green suite was reported as a failure, and telling that from a real one meant reading both blobs by hand. The run now says so explicitly when the merged report shows no failing test and a shard still exited non-zero, and names the two possibilities. **Read the merged file count against a known-good run before believing a red**: short counts mean a shard really died, matching counts mean the runner failed after the tests passed.
- **Test worlds are cached on disk** (`test/helpers/worldCache.ts`, used by `makeLeague`). vitest gives every test file its own worker process, so an in-process memo died at each file boundary and the same world was regenerated in every one of the ~69 files that wanted one — the single dominant cost in the suite. Worlds are now generated once ever into `node_modules/.cache/soccer-gm-worlds/<hash>/` and parsed back on demand. **Re-measured 2026-09-03 on the shipped 626-club/15,650-player world, on an idle machine: generation 6.8s, a cached load 110ms, 66ms on an in-process memo hit, 25.7 MB per fixture — so a cached world is ~60x cheaper than generating one, not free.** (The figures here read "~4.1s / ~12ms / ~340x / 36 files" until then, from the 240-club/6,000-player world three expansions ago; they were what people budgeted against when deciding a per-test load was harmless, and a 2026-09-03 audit found ~70 scattered per-`it` loads plus files loading a world for code that never reads a player. Note also that generation measures 6.8s solo against the 12-20s a contended run implies — the same solo-vs-contended gap the cluster entry warns about.) **A call still allocates 15,650 player objects, so hoist it to one shared fixture when a file's tests only read, and prefer an existing seed to a new one: every distinct `(userTid, seed, genSeed)` is another 25.7 MB fixture to regenerate after any `src/core` change.** **Always build test worlds with `makeLeague(tid, seed)`, never `createLeagueState` directly** — the one exception is a test that *reuses its rng after generation* (threading it into a later `simThrough`/`simOffseason`), which needs the rng advanced by generation and so must keep generating; those live in `international.test.ts`, `offseason.test.ts`, `simThrough.test.ts`, `transfersRender.test.tsx`, the m3/m4 validation gates and one case in `loans.test.ts`. Gotchas: **(a)** the cache key is a content hash of **all of `src/core`** plus `src/engine/rng.ts` and the Node version — deliberately broad, because generation cannot then depend on anything unhashed. Narrowing it to generation's current import list reintroduces silent staleness; don't. A core edit costs one rebuild (~60s) and is free thereafter. **(b)** It is only sound because generation is deterministic apart from `meta.created` (a wall-clock stamp) — `fixtureFidelity.test.ts` asserts both that and cached-equals-fresh, and is what stops the cache drifting silently. **(c)** `SOCCER_GM_NO_FIXTURE_CACHE=1` bypasses disk entirely if you ever suspect the cache. **(d) Nothing prunes it, so it grows by one directory per source state the machine has ever tested — measured 3.6 GB across 20 hashes, the largest 720 MB.** `npm run test:clean-worlds` (`scripts/pruneWorldCache.ts`) keeps the current hash plus anything touched in the last 7 days; `--all` keeps only the current one. Deliberately a command rather than something `loadFixture` does on its own: several worktrees share one cache directory and each may sit on a different hash, so self-pruning would have them continually destroying each other's fixtures and paying full generation again.

## Core invariants & gotchas (durable — read before touching sim code)

- **RNG stream order is load-bearing.** The sim is seeded/deterministic; inserting or removing an `rng` draw shifts every downstream roll (different ratings, names, results). When adding per-player traits or post-hoc attribution, derive them from a *separate* seeded stream (`mulberry32`/`hashInts` off pids/match-intrinsics), never from the shared `rng`. This has caused real bugs (AI deficits, shifted generation) multiple times — see any "RNG-stream-order" mention in git history.
- **OVR scale:** 65 ≈ average starter, 70 ≈ good starter, 75 ≈ a team's best, 80-85 ≈ league-wide elite, 90+ ≈ rare outlier. `OVR_WEIGHTS` (`players/templates.ts`) drives both generation and progression's `computeOvr`.
- **OVR is calibrated to mean the same thing at every position, and it takes TWO constants because two independent mechanisms break it (2026-08-23).** A position's OVR weights sit on the very skills its generation tiers roll highest — that is *what makes them* its key skills — so the weighting and `GEN_OFFSETS` amplify each other. Measured on a fresh world before the fix: mean OVR ST 55.9 down to FB 49.4, and **132 of 320 clubs had a striker as their best player against 1 with a full-back and 6 with a centre-mid** (expected ~38/51/51 at their roster shares). Full-back is the only position with no `star` skill; striker and winger have two each. The fix is *not* a reweight — the rows are read by the sim's slot logic, secondary positions and the position-change check, so flattening them would trade a true statement about football for arithmetic. Instead: **(1) `POSITION_OVR_CALIBRATION` (levels)** — an additive per-position shift, **zero-sum weighted by `ROSTER_COMPOSITION`**, so the world mean OVR is untouched and `LEAGUE_BASE` / `GROWTH_DAMPING_START` / `DIVISION_2_REFUSAL_OVR_THRESHOLD` / `PROTECTED_STAR_OVR` / the wage and valuation curves all still mean what they did. **(2) `POSITION_RATING_SPREAD` (spread)** — OVR is a weighted *mean* of independent draws, so its noise is `RATING_NOISE_SD × sqrt(Σ(w/W)²)`: a row concentrated on few attributes varies more and wins every extreme. Keeper is the outlier (goalkeeping is over half his weighting, 0.58 vs 0.34-0.42 outfield), and fixing only the levels handed him the problem instead — **GK went 68 → 103 of 320 clubs**. It scales the *generation draw* rather than the weight row, because the sim's `keeping` composite **is** the goalkeeping rating (`league/matchPlayers.ts`) and the AI prices keepers off OVR, so a diluted row would make OVR a worse read on the one thing he does; composites are z-normalized per competition, so a uniform change in a position's raw spread is nearly invisible to the football. **Derived at module load from `OVR_WEIGHTS`**, so editing a row re-derives it instead of silently reopening the gap. `computeOvr` also now **normalizes** skill weights (a row states relative importance only — GK's 92-sum hand-correction is obsolete and was also compressing keepers' slope against team strength) and **centres height** on the position's own range (a level effect wearing a skill weight's clothes). Result: every position within 0.3 OVR on mean, sd and p90, and club-best tracks roster share. Tools: `scripts/positionOvrCalibrate.ts` (prints the table), `scripts/positionOvrProbe.ts` (the symptom), `test/core/positionOvrBalance.test.ts` (the gate). **Three traps.** (a) **`SECONDARY_POSITION_CUTOFF` MUST be re-derived in the same change** — its bars are percentiles of a per-pair *gap*, so any level move shifts every bar touching that position; left alone, "holds a second position" ran CB 93% / DM 93% against FB 1%, i.e. the fixed share the design rests on had stopped being fixed. (b) **Measuring "best player at his club" needs a fair tie-break.** OVR is an integer and a squad's top few sit within a point or two, so awarding the tie to whoever comes first in the roster array biases the whole table by generation order — it read as a 4-point positional skew that wasn't there. (c) The 8×8 matrix of "mean OVR of position A's players evaluated at B" must keep its **diagonal maximal** (it does, before and after) — that is the check that the calibration didn't turn some position into a conversion magnet. `migrate.ts` re-derives `ovr` (and every `hist` snapshot's) from stored ratings on load, clamping `potential` up to it; the spread half applies at generation only, so old saves keep their original spread.
- **Anti-inflation (fragile equilibrium — several mechanics exist only to hold it):** youth intake anchors to a fixed-at-generation `academyBase`, never to the club's current roster average (the torn-out anchor caused unbounded ratchet). Potential is a *scout estimate* (`estimatePotential`), never a growth input. Every position's expected lifetime rating arc is ~0/slightly negative. `developmentBias` (persistent per-player trait, deterministic from pid) **must taper to exactly 0 by peak age**, or inflation reopens. `growthDamping` scales down only *positive* deltas as ovr climbs. `YOUTH_BASE_OFFSET` offsets the fact that `generatePlayer` doesn't vary quality by age. Any new mechanic that shifts these must be dynasty-audited (`scripts/`).
- **The user's club is unmanaged by design** in headless audits — no AI free agency/renewals/buys touch it, so it rots and produces *all* extreme worst-case roster/points stats. **Exclude `userTid` from tail metrics** in any audit, or report it separately.
- **Match composites are z-normalized within each competition.** A weak league reads as average in its own matches; cross-league surfaces (the Cup) must pool participants into a *shared* baseline (`simThrough`'s `cupMatchData`) or the weak club gets normalized back up to parity.
- **Composites bucket by SLOT, not natural position (`rollupComposites` takes `SlottedPlayer[]`, `engine/positionFit.ts`, 2026-08-12).** The team's shape comes from its formation, so every group and weight keys off the slot a player occupies; his own `pos` only decides what it *costs* him to be there (`familiarityPenalty`, in raw rating points: `POSITION_ADJACENT_PENALTY` 6 / `POSITION_FOREIGN_PENALTY` 16 / `POSITION_KEEPER_PENALTY` 35, all in `engine/constants.ts`). Before this, groups were built from `p.pos` and the slot was forgotten after selection, so **moving a defender up the pitch made your defense better** (he stayed in the defensive group at full strength and simply left the attacking one) and `control` had the same inverted sign. `MatchPlayer` now carries `slot`; a substitute **inherits the slot of the man he replaces** (via a *copy*, never mutation — `TeamMatchData` objects are shared across every match built from one `leagueMatchData` call, e.g. `season.ts` builds it once for all 380 fixtures, so mutating a slot would leak into unrelated matches). `slotPlayed` tracks the played slot for the box score, which is built from the *original* roster arrays where a sub still carries his bench slot. Attribution weights, `computeMatchRating`, and every "who's the keeper" lookup follow the slot too. **The load-bearing consumer is the substitution logic, not the XI** — measured, starters are 99.2% exact-fit and 0% foreign, but the bench was putting a player in a foreign slot on **37% of all subs** (a striker replacing a tired centre-back was the single most common swap) because `pickReplacement` was exact-pos-or-anyone. It now maximizes `benchValue - familiarityPenalty(slot, pos)`, and `worthSub` gates on the same penalized value; **those two must stay on one number**, or the sim makes swaps it then punishes. Result: foreign subs 37% → 0.2%, and ~19% fewer subs overall (holding a tired starter on beats wrecking the shape). Chase-the-game subs pass `reshape` to `commitSub` so the arriving attacker plays *his own* position (a genuine shape change, one fewer defender) rather than being stranded in a defensive slot. Also closes the documented `pickReplacement` GK gap: an outfielder forced into goal is now penalized instead of silently leaving `keeping` at a neutral 0.5 while counting as an outfielder. Pure function of attributes, **no shared-`rng` draw**, so stream order is untouched — but composite values change, hence results change, hence the `touchStats.test.ts` scoreline hash was rebased. Measured over 20 seeded seasons: champion pts 79.20 → 80.55, and *variance fell* (champion sd 5.24 → 3.77, bottom sd 6.49 → 3.99), since fewer matches are decided by a side dismantling its own shape late. **Dynasty-audited** (`scripts/slotChurnAudit.ts`, 15 seasons × 2 seeds) for the obvious risk — that punishing thin squads sharpens the strong/weak gap into a procession — and it went the *other* way: distinct champions per league 8.0/8.1 → 9.4/9.3, dominant club's title share 33.3%/31.7% → 28.3%/23.3%, champion pts flat (77.9/78.1 → 78.1/77.4), ovr drift still ~0 (anti-inflation intact), D2 ceiling unchanged within noise. Title races got *more* open, because a side can no longer stumble into a lopsided shape that its raw quality doesn't justify.
- **Secondary positions are DERIVED, never stored (`core/players/positions.ts`, `COVERABLE` in `engine/positionFit.ts`, 2026-08-13).** A player also plays any slot he can cover where his OVR there beats his own by at least `SECONDARY_POSITION_CUTOFF[pos][slot]`. A secondary is an **exact fit** (`fitTier` returns 0), so it waives `familiarityPenalty` entirely *and* makes him a first-choice `selectXI` candidate for that slot — a 74 winger who really does play full-back now takes the slot off a 66 full-back, where before fit was strictly by listed position. Threaded onto `MatchPlayer.secondary` because the derivation needs core's per-position OVR model and the engine can't import core; `benchValueAt` is the single sub-side caller, so `worthSub`/`pickReplacement` stay on one number by construction. Pure, **no rng draw** (stream order untouched), no persisted field, no migration. Memoised in a `WeakMap` keyed on the **player object** — the core hands unchanged players through by reference, so a progressed player is a new object and misses the cache automatically; a pid key would go stale the season he develops.
  - **`COVERABLE` is the REVERSE of `ADJACENCY`, and getting it backwards is silent.** `ADJACENCY[X]` lists who can cover *slot X*; "where else can this player play" is the transpose. The table is **not** symmetric, so a forward lookup is wrong for exactly the pairs that matter and right everywhere else: a DM can cover at full-back but a full-back cannot cover in defensive midfield, and a winger can play up front but a striker is not thereby an attacking mid. Derived from `ADJACENCY` at module load so the two can't drift; a test pins the transpose and a second test pins the asymmetric pairs, so it can't quietly degrade into "same thing twice".
  - **One shared OVR threshold cannot work at ANY value — measured, and it was the first design.** How close a player rates one position along is a property of the position PAIR: CM/DM/AM share most of their OVR weighting, a striker's overlaps almost nothing. At a 3-point gap that gives **100% of CMs and 6% of STs**; there is no value where both are sane. `SECONDARY_POSITION_CUTOFF` is therefore per-pair, measured to admit `SECONDARY_POSITION_RATE` (0.15) of each position's players. Regenerate with `scripts/secondaryPositionProbe.ts` after any change to the OVR weights, the adjacency table or the rate.
